\displaystyle\epsilon_{i}\equiv\frac{\dot{\epsilon}_{i-1}}{H\epsilon_{i-1}}\,,\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \textrm{with:}\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \epsilon_{0}\equiv\frac{1}{H}\,.

(6.1)

The relations with the usual inflationary parameters are
\epsilon\equiv\epsilon_{1}=-\dot{H}/H^{2}
and instead of
\eta
we have the second Hubble parameters
1
1

1

We remark that in ref.
[
683
]
the symbol
\eta
refers to the second Hubble parameter
\epsilon_{2}
.

\displaystyle\eta\equiv-\frac{\ddot{H}}{2H\dot{H}}=\epsilon-\frac{1}{2}\frac{d\log\epsilon}{dN}\,,\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \textrm{with:}\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \epsilon_{2}=2\epsilon-2\eta\,.

(6.2)

We consider the theory described by the action

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}=\int d^{4}x\sqrt{-g}\bigg[\frac{1}{2}R(g)+\frac{1}{2}g^{\mu\nu}(\partial_{\mu}\phi)(\partial_{\nu}\phi)-V(\phi)\bigg]\,.

(6.3)

R(g)
is the scalar curvature associated with the space-time whose geometry is described by the metric
g
with line element

ds^{2}=g_{\mu\nu}dx^{\mu}dx^{\nu}
.
The classical background evolves in the flat FLRW Universe and the background value of the scalar field is a function of time,
\phi(t)
.
We tacitly assume that the scalar potential features an approximate stationary inflection point so as to trigger the transition SR/USR/SR.

We focus on scalar perturbations.
We consider the perturbed metric in the following generic form

\displaystyle ds^{2}=N^{2}dt^{2}-h_{ij}(N^{i}dt+dx^{i})(N^{j}dt+dx^{j})\,,

(6.4)

and choose the gauge in which

\displaystyle N=1+\delta N(\vec{x},t)\,,\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ N^{i}=\delta^{ij}\partial_{j}B(\vec{x},t)\,,\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ h_{ij}=a^{2}(t)e^{2\zeta(\vec{x},t)}\delta_{ij}\,,\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \delta\phi(\vec{x},t)=0\,.

(6.5)

The field
\zeta(\vec{x},t)
is the only independent scalar degree of freedom since

N
and
N^{i}
are Lagrange multipliers subject to the momentum and Hamiltonian constraints.
It is important to stress that the variable
\zeta
as defined in eq. (
6.5
) is constant outside the horizon and represents the correct non-linear generalization of the Bardeen variable
[
684
]
.

At the quadratic order in the fluctuations, the action is

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{2}=\int d^{4}x\,\epsilon\,a^{3}\left[\dot{\zeta}^{2}-\frac{(\partial_{k}\zeta)(\partial^{k}\zeta)}{a^{2}}\right]\,.

(6.6)

Comoving curvature perturbations are quantized by introducing the free operator

\displaystyle\hat{\zeta}(\vec{x},\tau)=\int\frac{d^{3}\vec{k}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\hat{\zeta}(\vec{k},\tau)e^{i\vec{x}\cdot\vec{k}}\,,\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \textrm{with:}\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \hat{\zeta}(\vec{k},\tau)=\zeta_{k}(\tau)a_{\vec{k}}+\zeta_{k}^{*}(\tau)a^{{\dagger}}_{-\vec{k}}\,,

(6.7)

and

[a_{\vec{k}},a_{\vec{k}^{\prime}}]=[a_{\vec{k}}^{{\dagger}},a_{\vec{k}^{\prime}}^{{\dagger}}]=0\,,\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ [a_{\vec{k}},a_{\vec{k}^{\prime}}^{{\dagger}}]=(2\pi)^{3}\delta^{(3)}(\vec{k}-\vec{k}^{\prime})\,,\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ a_{\vec{k}}|0\rangle=0\,,

(6.8)

where the last condition defines the vacuum of the free theory
|0\rangle
. We define the comoving wavenumber
k\equiv|\vec{k}|
.
The scale factor in the FLRW Universe corresponds to a rescaling of the spatial coordinate; consequently, physically sensible results should be invariant under
the rescaling
[
685
]

\displaystyle a\to\lambda a\,,\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \vec{x}\to\vec{x}/\lambda\,,\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \vec{k}\to\lambda\vec{k}\,,\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ k\to|\lambda|k\,,\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ {\textrm{with}}\penalty\ \penalty\ \lambda\in\mathbb{R}\,.

(6.9)

Furthermore, if we consider the conformal time
\tau
instead of the cosmic time
t
, we also have

\displaystyle\tau\to\tau/\lambda\,.

(6.10)

Notice that, under the above rescaling, we have
a_{\vec{k}}\to a_{\vec{k}}/|\lambda|^{3/2}
(from the scaling property of the three-dimensional
\delta
function) and, consequently,
\zeta_{k}\to\zeta_{k}/|\lambda|^{3/2}
so that
\hat{\zeta}(\vec{k},\tau)\to\hat{\zeta}(\vec{k},\tau)/|\lambda|^{3}
and
\hat{\zeta}(\vec{x},\tau)
invariant.
In the case of free fields, we have

\displaystyle\langle 0|\hat{\zeta}(\vec{k}_{1},\tau_{1})\hat{\zeta}(\vec{k}_{2},\tau_{2})|0\rangle=(2\pi)^{3}\delta(\vec{k}_{1}+\vec{k}_{2})\zeta_{k_{1}}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{k_{2}}^{*}(\tau_{2})\,.

(6.11)

In the presence of a time-derivative, we simply have

\displaystyle\langle 0|\hat{\zeta}^{\prime}(\vec{k}_{1},\tau_{1})\hat{\zeta}(\vec{k}_{2},\tau_{2})|0\rangle=(2\pi)^{3}\delta(\vec{k}_{1}+\vec{k}_{2})\zeta^{\prime}_{k_{1}}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{k_{2}}^{*}(\tau_{2})\,.

(6.12)

Note that the time dependence occurs in the
mode function, not in the raising/lowering
operator. The mode function
\zeta_{k}(\tau)
is related to the linear-order Mukhanov-Sasaki (M-S) equation. More in detail, if we define
\zeta_{k}(\tau)=u_{k}(\tau)/z(\tau)
with
z(\tau)\equiv a(\tau)\sqrt{2\epsilon(\tau)}
, the mode
u_{k}(\tau)
verifies the equation

\displaystyle\frac{d^{2}u_{k}}{d\tau^{2}}+\left(k^{2}-\frac{1}{z}\frac{d^{2}z}{d\tau^{2}}\right)u_{k}=0\,.

(6.13)

We are interested in the computation of the quantity

\displaystyle\lim_{\tau\to 0^{-}}\langle\hat{\zeta}(\vec{x}_{1},\tau)\hat{\zeta}(\vec{x}_{2},\tau)\rangle=\int\frac{d^{3}\vec{k}}{(2\pi)^{3}}P(k)e^{i\vec{k}\cdot(\vec{x}_{1}-\vec{x}_{2})}\,,

(6.14)

at some late time
\tau\to 0^{-}
at which curvature perturbations become constant at super-horizon scales. Equivalently, we write

\displaystyle\lim_{\tau\to 0^{-}}\langle\hat{\zeta}(\vec{x},\tau)\hat{\zeta}(\vec{x},\tau)\rangle=\int\frac{dk}{k}\underbrace{\left[\frac{k^{3}}{2\pi^{2}}P(k)\right]}_{\equiv\mathcal{P}(k)}=\int\frac{dk}{k}\mathcal{P}(k)\,,

(6.15)

where
\mathcal{P}(k)
is the a-dimensional power spectrum.
At the level of the quadratic action, we find

\displaystyle\langle 0|\hat{\zeta}(\vec{x}_{1},\tau)\hat{\zeta}(\vec{x}_{2},\tau)|0\rangle

\displaystyle=\int\frac{d^{3}\vec{k}_{1}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\,d^{3}\vec{k}_{2}\delta(\vec{k}_{1}+\vec{k}_{2})\,\zeta_{k_{1}}(\tau)\zeta_{k_{2}}^{*}(\tau)e^{i(\vec{x}_{1}\cdot k_{1}+\vec{x}_{2}\cdot k_{2})}=\int\frac{d^{3}\vec{k}}{(2\pi)^{3}}|\zeta_{k}(\tau)|^{2}e^{i\vec{k}\cdot(\vec{x}_{1}-\vec{x}_{2})}\,,

(6.16)

which of course gives the familiar result

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}(k)=\lim_{\tau\to 0^{-}}\frac{k^{3}}{2\pi^{2}}|\zeta_{k}(\tau)|^{2}\,.

(6.17)

Figure 6.1:

Classical (top panel) and quantum (central panel) dynamics in the context of an explicit single-field model of inflation that exhibits the presence of a phase of USR in between the time interval
N_{\textrm{\rm in}}<N<N_{\textrm{\rm end}}
(cf. ref.
[
231
]
). In this specific realization,
we have
N_{\textrm{\rm in}}=36.3
and

N_{\textrm{\rm end}}=38.8
.

Top panel:
we plot the evolution of the background quantities
\epsilon
,
\epsilon_{2}
and
\epsilon_{2}^{\prime}
(cf. eqs. (
6.1
,
6.2
)) together with the evolution of the Hubble rate (normalized with respect to the value
H_{*}\equiv H(N_{*})
and scaled by a factor 10 to ease of comparison).

Central panel:
we plot the solutions of the M-S equation in eq. (
6.24
) for two different curvature modes.
The mode in black (blue) exits the Hubble horizon well before (during) the USR phase.

Bottom panel:
we plot the classicality parameter
C_{k}
for the same two modes (cf. the main text for details).
In the case of the black mode (
N_{k}\ll N_{\rm in}
) the classicality parameter quickly vanishes after horizon crossing, and remains negligible also during the USR phase. In the case of the blue mode (
N_{q}\approx N_{\rm end}
) the classicality parameter remains sizable during the USR phase, indicating that this mode retains its quantum nature during USR.

In order to make our discussion more concrete, we can refer to fig.
6.1
, see the caption for details. In this figure, we plot both the classical (top panel) and quantum (bottom panel) dynamics that characterize a realistic model of single-field inflation that features a phase of USR because of the presence of an approximate stationary inflection point in the inflationary potential. For more details about the model, see ref.,
[
231
]
. Notably, without including loop corrections to the curvature power spectrum computation, this model is compatible with CMB constraints and predicts
\approx 100\%
of dark matter in the form of asteroid-mass PBHs
2
2

2

The model predicts the tensor-to-scalar ratio
r\simeq 0.037
which is still (barely) compatible, at 95% confidence level, with the latest results released by the BICEP and Keck collaboration
[
686
]
.
.
In fig.
6.1
, the relation between
e
-fold time
N
(bottom
x
-axis) and conformal time
\tau
(top
x
-axis) is given by the integral

\displaystyle\tau=-\frac{1}{k_{\star}}\int_{N}^{N_{0}}\frac{H(N_{\star})}{H(N^{\prime})}e^{N_{\star}-N^{\prime}}dN^{\prime}\,,

(6.18)

where
N_{0}
indicates the end of inflation, with
\tau
conventionally set to
0
at this time, and
N_{\star}
the instant of time at which the comoving scale
k_{\star}=0.05
Mpc
-1
crosses the Hubble horizon. In fig.
6.1
, we set
N_{\star}=0
, and the model gives
N_{0}-N_{\star}\simeq 52
.
We can highlight a few crucial properties of the dynamics presented above:

a)

We start from the classical analysis. During USR,

\epsilon_{2}(\tau)
changes according to the schematic

\displaystyle\epsilon_{2}\approx 0\ \ \overset{\textrm{SR/USR at time }\tau_{\textrm{in}}}{\Longrightarrow}\ \ |\epsilon_{2}|>3\ \ \overset{\textrm{USR/SR at time }\tau_{\textrm{end}}}{\Longrightarrow}\ \ \epsilon_{2}\approx O(1)\,,

(6.19)

thus making
\epsilon_{2}^{\prime}(\tau)
non-zero at around the two transitions at conformal times
\tau_{\textrm{in}}
and
\tau_{\textrm{end}}
(equivalently, at
e
-fold times
N_{\textrm{in}}
and
N_{\textrm{end}}
).
The evolution of
\epsilon_{2}
and
\epsilon^{\prime}_{2}
are shown in the top panel of fig.
6.1
, with dotted green and dashed orange lines, respectively.

b)

During USR,
the Hubble parameter
\epsilon
decreases exponentially fast (the inflaton almost stops its classical motion).
The evolution of
\epsilon
is shown in the top panel of fig.
6.1
(dot-dashed magenta line); in addition, we also plot the time evolution of the Hubble rate
H
.

c)

We now consider the USR dynamics at the quantum level.
It is crucial to understand the typical behavior of curvature modes
(solid lines in the central panel of fig.
6.1
) and their time derivatives (dashed lines).
In the central panel of fig.
6.1
, we plot two representative cases: the black lines correspond to the case of a mode
\zeta_{k}
that exits the Hubble horizon at some time
N_{k}
well before the USR phase (like a CMB mode) while the blue lines correspond to a curvature mode
\zeta_{q}
that exits the Hubble horizon at some time
N_{q}
during the USR phase.
We notice that the derivative
|d\zeta_{k}/dN|
decays exponentially fast,
and, soon after horizon crossing, becomes negligible, while
|\zeta_{k}|
settles to a constant value.
Consequently, we expect that interaction terms that involve the time derivative of CMB modes will be strongly suppressed.

d)

Finally, we consider the issue of the quantum-to-classical transition. We define the so-called classicality parameter
[
687
]

C_{k}=|\zeta_{k}\dot{\zeta}_{k}^{*}-\zeta_{k}^{*}\dot{\zeta}_{k}|/|\zeta_{k}\dot{\zeta}_{k}|
which goes to zero in the classical limit.
In the case of conventional SR dynamics,
the classicality parameter scales according to
C_{k}\sim 2k\tau
, and it vanishes exponentially fast right after the horizon crossing time.
In the bottom panel of fig.
6.1
, we plot the classicality parameter for two representative modes of our dynamics.
The black modes experiences its horizon crossing well before the USR phase (
N_{k}\ll N_{\rm in}
). Its classicality parameter quickly vanishes and remains
\ll 1
during the subsequent USR phase. The blue line, on the contrary, represents the classicality parameter for a mode that experiences its horizon crossing during the USR phase. Its classicality parameter remains sizable during the USR phase, indicating that this short mode can not be treated classically during USR.

As a warm-up exercise, we now introduce a simple semi-analytical model called
reverse engineering approach

[
326
,
244
,
269
]
.
We define the hyperbolic tangent parametrization

\displaystyle\eta(N)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left[-\eta_{\rm II}+\eta_{\rm II}\tanh\left(\frac{N-N_{\rm in}}{\delta N}\right)\right]+\frac{1}{2}\left[\eta_{\rm II}+\eta_{\rm III}+(\eta_{\rm III}-\eta_{\rm II})\tanh\left(\frac{N-N_{\rm end}}{\delta N}\right)\right]\,,

(6.20)

where the parameter
\delta N
controls the width of the two transitions at
N_{\rm in}
and
N_{\rm end}
.
The limit
\delta N\to 0
reproduces the step-function approximation.
Using the definition

\displaystyle\delta(x)=\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\frac{1}{2\epsilon\cosh^{2}(x/\epsilon)}\,,

(6.21)

we find

\displaystyle\lim_{\delta N\to 0}\frac{d\eta}{dN}=(-\eta_{\rm II}+\eta_{\rm III})\delta(N-N_{\rm end})+\eta_{\rm II}\delta(N-N_{\rm in})\,.

(6.22)

Using
\eta\simeq-(1/2)d\log\epsilon/dN
, we find the following expression

\displaystyle\frac{\epsilon(N)}{\epsilon_{\textrm{ref}}}=

\displaystyle e^{-\eta_{\rm III}(N-N_{\rm ref})}\left[\cosh\left(\frac{N-N_{\rm end}}{\delta N}\right)\cosh\left(\frac{N-N_{\rm in}}{\delta N}\right)\right]^{-\frac{\delta N\eta_{\rm III}}{2}}\times

\displaystyle\left[\cosh\left(\frac{N_{\rm ref}-N_{\rm end}}{\delta N}\right)\cosh\left(\frac{N_{\rm ref}-N_{\rm in}}{\delta N}\right)\right]^{\frac{\delta N\eta_{\rm III}}{2}}\times

\displaystyle\left[\cosh\left(\frac{N-N_{\rm end}}{\delta N}\right){\rm sech}\left(\frac{N-N_{\rm in}}{\delta N}\right)\right]^{\delta N\left(\eta_{\rm II}-\frac{\eta_{\rm III}}{2}\right)}\times

\displaystyle\left[\cosh\left(\frac{N_{\rm ref}-N_{\rm end}}{\delta N}\right){\rm sech}\left(\frac{N_{\rm ref}-N_{\rm in}}{\delta N}\right)\right]^{\frac{\delta N}{2}(-2\eta_{\rm II}+\eta_{\rm III})},\,

(6.23)

where
\epsilon_{\textrm{ref}}\ll 1
is the value of
\epsilon
at some initial reference time
N_{\textrm{ref}}
.
For future reference, we define
\bar{\epsilon}(N)\equiv\epsilon(N)/\epsilon_{\textrm{ref}}
.

Figure 6.2:

Schematic evolution of
\eta(N)
in eq. (
6.20
) (
top panel
),
\epsilon(N)
in eq. (
6.23
) (
central panel
)
and
d\epsilon_{2}/dN
(
bottom panel
)
as function of the number
of
e
-folds
N
. We explore different values of
\delta N
with the limit
\delta N\to 0
that corresponds to instantaneous transitions SR/USR at
N=N_{\textrm{\rm in}}
and USR/SR at
N=N_{\textrm{\rm end}}
.
In the right panel, the limit
\delta N\to 0
corresponds to
\delta
function transitions at
N_{\textrm{\rm in}}
and
N_{\textrm{\rm end}}
. Furthermore, notice that the lines corresponding to
d\epsilon_{2}/dN
and
-2d\eta/dN
superimpose, showing that
-2d\eta/dN
is a perfect approximation of
d\epsilon_{2}/dN
.

In this way we have an analytical description of the background dynamics; most importantly, eqs. (
6.20
,
6.23
) are almost all that we need to know to solve
the M-S equation
[
16
,
17
]

\displaystyle\frac{d^{2}u_{k}}{dN^{2}}

\displaystyle+(1-\epsilon)\frac{du_{k}}{dN}+\left[\frac{k^{2}}{(aH)^{2}}+(1+\epsilon-\eta)(\eta-2)-\frac{d}{dN}(\epsilon-\eta)\right]u_{k}=0\,.

(6.24)

For consistency with eq. (
6.23
), we consider the Hubble rate as a function of time according to

\displaystyle H(N)=H(N_{\textrm{ref}})\exp\left[-\int_{N_{\textrm{ref}}}^{N}\epsilon(N^{\prime})dN^{\prime}\right]\,.

(6.25)

We shall use the short-hand notation

a(N_{\textrm{i}})\equiv a_{\textrm{i}}
and

H(N_{\textrm{i}})\equiv H_{\textrm{i}}
.
Consequently,
we rewrite eq. (
6.24
) in the form

\displaystyle\frac{d^{2}u_{k}}{dN^{2}}

\displaystyle+(1-\epsilon)\frac{du_{k}}{dN}+\left[\bar{k}^{2}\bigg(\frac{H_{\textrm{in}}}{H}\bigg)^{2}e^{2(N_{\textrm{in}}-N)}+(1+\epsilon-\eta)(\eta-2)-\frac{d}{dN}(\epsilon-\eta)\right]u_{k}=0\,,

(6.26)

with
\bar{k}\equiv{k}/({a_{\textrm{in}}H_{\textrm{in}}})
.
We solve the above equation for different
\bar{k}
with Bunch-Davies initial conditions

\displaystyle\sqrt{k}\,u_{k}(N)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\,,\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \sqrt{k}\,\frac{du_{k}}{dN}(N)=-\frac{i}{\sqrt{2}}\frac{k}{a(N)H(N)}\,,

(6.27)

at some arbitrary time
N\ll N_{k}
with
k=a(N_{k})H(N_{k})
.
Modes with
\bar{k}\approx O(1)
are modes that exit the Hubble horizon at about the beginning of the USR phase,
modes with
\bar{k}\ll 1
are modes that exit the Hubble horizon well before the beginning of the USR phase,
modes with
\bar{k}\gg 1
are modes that exit the Hubble horizon well after the beginning of the USR phase.
In the left panel of fig.
6.3
,
we show the tree-level power spectrum that we obtain by numerically solving eq. (
6.26
) and using eq. (
6.17
).
Thanks to our parametrization in eq. (
6.20
) we control the sharpness of the transition varying
\delta N
.

Figure 6.3:

Left panel:

Tree-level power spectrum using the reverse engineering approach.
The numerical values of the other parameters are

\eta_{\textrm{\rm II}}=3.5
,

\eta_{\textrm{\rm III}}=0
and

N_{\textrm{\rm end}}-N_{\textrm{\rm in}}=2.5
.
In our parametrization, we go beyond the instantaneous transition approximation and
we explore different values of
\delta N
.
The vertical double-arrow indicates the growth of the power spectrum
given by the naïve scaling

\Delta\mathcal{P}=(k_{\rm end}/k_{\rm in})^{2\eta_{\rm II}}=e^{2\eta_{\rm II}\Delta N_{\rm{USR}}}
.
This scaling captures well the amplitude of the transition from the initial to the final SR phase but it does not give a reliable estimate of the peak amplitude of the power spectrum, which can easily be one order of magnitude larger.

Right panel:

Time evolution of two representative modes with
\bar{k}=1
and
\bar{k}=e^{\Delta N_{\rm{USR}}}
for
\delta N\in[0.1-0.5]
(from darker to lighter colors, respectively).
The black lines represent the limit
\delta N\to 0
.

6.2
One-loop issue in USR models

A very legitimate question is whether the
USR dynamics is consistent with perturbativity.
Technically speaking, the dimensionless power spectrum of curvature perturbation
\mathcal{P}(k)
is typically computed within the free theory. However, curvature perturbations, being gravitational in nature, feature an intricate architecture of non-linear interactions. The effect of non-linear interactions is twofold.
On the one hand, they generate, in addition to the variance, non-zero higher-order cumulants that may leave a peculiar non-Gaussian pattern to the statistics of the curvature field.
On the other hand, the variance itself gets corrected with respect to the value computed within the free theory.
We focus on the second effect and, on very general grounds, we define the perturbative criterion

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}(k)\equiv\mathcal{P}_{\rm tree}(k)\left[1+\Delta{\cal P}_{\rm 1-loop}(k)\right]\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \Delta{\cal P}_{\rm 1-loop}(k)\overset{!}{<}1\,,

(6.28)

meaning that the power spectrum computed within the free theory (the “tree-level” power spectrum in the above equation) must be larger than the corrections
\Delta\mathcal{P}
introduced by the presence of interactions. Such corrections can be organized in a formal series expansion, and
we will focus in particular on the first-order term, dubbed “1-loop” in the above equation.

The gut feeling is that, unless one is led to consider
\mathcal{P}_{\rm tree}(k)=O(1)
, perturbativity should be under control.
However, in
ref.
[
683
]
when it firstly appeared on the ArXiv in November 2022, the authors made the bold claim that, in the presence of USR, the perturbativity condition in eq. (
6.28
) could be violated at scales
k
relevant for
CMB observations; even more strikingly, initially ref.
[
683
]
argues that USR dynamics
tailored to generate a sizable abundance of asteroid- or solar-mass PBHs are ruled-out
3
3

3

In the published version of ref.
[
683
]
, appeared one year later than ref.
[
2
]
the authors change the claim of the paper stressing that there is a constraints on the USR models but in realistic scenarios they are not ruled out, finding, where possible, comparable results respect the analysis presented in this chapter.
.
What makes the initial claim of ref.
[
683
]
so hard to accept is that it basically says that
loop of short modes alters the correlation of long CMB modes.
This is counter-intuitive since it clashes with the intuition that physics at different scales should be decoupled.

Given the above, it is not surprising that ref.
[
683
]
sparked an intense debate mostly exclusively polarized on defending or disproving the claim that PBH formation from single-field inflation is ruled out
[
688
,
689
,
690
,
691
,
692
,
693
,
694
,
695
,
696
]
.
Here we critically examine the consequences of
eq. (
6.28
) in the presence of single-field inflation with USR dynamics, taking into account a proper computations of the PBH abundance and going beyond the instantaneous transition approximation used in ref.
[
683
]
.

In order to make contact with the analysis of ref.
[
683
]
, we use the
reverse engineering approach
(eq.
6.20
) and we set
\eta_{\rm III}=0
.
However, it should be noted that
in more realistic models we need
\eta_{\textrm{III}}\neq 0
and negative so that the power spectrum decreases for modes with
\bar{k}\gg 1
. This feature is necessary if we want to connect the USR phase to a subsequent SR phase that ends inflation.
Since we are considering single-field models of inflation, in our analysis this is a necessary requirement.
Consequently, the power spectrum at small scales – both before and after the peak – does not respect the property of scale invariance.
Before the peak, the power spectrum
of the short modes grows with a maximum slope given by
\mathcal{P}(\bar{k})\sim\bar{k}^{4}
; after the peak, the power spectrum
of the short modes decays approximately as
\mathcal{P}(\bar{k})\sim\bar{k}^{2\eta_{\textrm{III}}}
.
After the peak, therefore, the power spectrum becomes approximately scale invariant only
if we take
\eta_{\textrm{III}}\approx 0
;
however, in such case
\epsilon
remains anchored to the tiny value reached during the USR phase and inflation never ends.

In ref.
[
683
]
,
the loop integration is restricted to the interval of modes
\bar{k}\in[\bar{k}_{\textrm{in}},\bar{k}_{\textrm{end}}]
where

\bar{k}_{\textrm{in}}=1

and

\bar{k}_{\textrm{end}}=e^{\Delta N_{\textrm{USR}}}(H_{\textrm{end}}/H_{\textrm{in}})\simeq e^{\Delta N_{\textrm{USR}}}

with
\Delta N_{\textrm{USR}}\equiv N_{\textrm{end}}-N_{\textrm{in}}
.
This interval of modes is limited by the two vertical dashed lines in the left panel of fig.
6.3
.
In ref.
[
683
]
, limiting the integration to the range
\bar{k}\in[\bar{k}_{\textrm{in}},\bar{k}_{\textrm{end}}]
is justified by the fact that the power spectrum of short modes peaks in this window of modes.

For future reference, let us stress one more important point. In the left panel of fig.
6.3
we indicate the growth of the power spectrum given by the scaling
\Delta\mathcal{P}=(k_{\rm end}/k_{\rm in})^{2\eta_{\rm II}}
.
This result immediately follows from the application of the SR formula

\mathcal{P}(k)=H^{2}/8\pi^{2}\epsilon
if one accounts for the exponential decay
\epsilon\sim e^{-2\eta_{\rm II}N}
during USR and converts
N
into
k
by means of the horizon-crossing condition
k=aH
.
Therefore, not surprisingly, the scaling

\Delta\mathcal{P}=(k_{\rm end}/k_{\rm in})^{2\eta_{\rm II}}
captures well the growth of the power spectrum if one directly jumps from the initial to the final SR phase.
However, as shown in the left panel of fig.
6.3
, the above estimate does not accurately describe the amplitude of the power spectrum at the position of its peak; the latter can easily be one order of magnitude larger than what suggested by
\Delta\mathcal{P}=(k_{\rm end}/k_{\rm in})^{2\eta_{\rm II}}
.
This features has important consequences when estimating the PBH abundance, which rather sensitive to the spectral amplitude.

Finally, it is possible to check numerically that neglecting the time dependence of the Hubble rate as in eq. (
6.25
) has a negligible impact.
In the following, therefore, we shall keep
H
constant (that is,
H=H_{\textrm{ref}}
does not evolve in time).
Furthermore, if we take
H
constant and in the limit
\delta N=0
, it is possible to get, for some special values of
\eta_{\textrm{II}}
and
\eta_{\textrm{III}}
, a complete analytical description of the SR/USR/SR dynamics
[
326
,
231
]
.

6.2.1
The interaction terms

The goal is to compute corrections that arise from the presence of interactions.
This means that, in
eq. (
6.14
), the vacuum expectation value should refer to the vacuum
|\Omega\rangle
of the interacting theory and the dynamics of the operator
\hat{\zeta}(\vec{x},\tau)
are described by the full action that also includes interactions.

We compute the left-hand side of eq. (
6.14
) by means of the “
in
-
in
” formalism (see e.g.
[
697
,
698
,
699
]
). Correlators are given by

\displaystyle\langle\Omega|\hat{\mathcal{O}}(\tau)|\Omega\rangle\equiv\langle\hat{\mathcal{O}}(\tau)\rangle=\langle 0|\left\{\bar{T}\exp\left[{i\int_{-\infty(1+i\epsilon)}^{\tau}d\tau^{\prime}\hat{H}_{\textrm{int}}(\tau^{\prime})}\right]\right\}\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{I}(\tau)\left\{T\exp\left[{-i\int_{-\infty(1-i\epsilon)}^{\tau}d\tau^{\prime}\hat{H}_{\textrm{int}}(\tau^{\prime})}\right]\right\}|0\rangle\,,

(6.29)

where on the right-hand side all fields appearing in
\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{I}(\tau)
and
\hat{H}_{\textrm{int}}(\tau^{\prime})
are free fields in the interaction picture.
We shall indicate free fields in the interaction picture with the additional subscript
I
.
It should be noted that the latter are nothing but the operators of the free theory that we quantized in eq. (
6.7
).

T
and
\bar{T}
are the time and anti-time ordering operator, respectively.
As customary, the small imaginary deformation in the integration contour
guarantees that
|\Omega\rangle\to|0\rangle
as
\tau\to-\infty
where
|\Omega\rangle
is the vacuum of the interacting theory.
On the left-hand side, the operator
\hat{\mathcal{O}}(\tau)
is the equal-time product of operators at different space
points, precisely like in eq. (
6.14
).
We expand in the interaction Hamiltonian, so we use the Dyson series

\displaystyle T\exp\left[-i\int_{-\infty_{-}}^{\tau}d\tau^{\prime}\hat{H}_{\textrm{int}}(\tau^{\prime})\right]=1-i\int_{-\infty_{-}}^{\tau}d\tau^{\prime}\hat{H}_{\textrm{int}}(\tau^{\prime})+i^{2}\int_{-\infty_{-}}^{\tau}d\tau^{\prime}\int_{-\infty_{-}}^{\tau^{\prime}}d\tau^{\prime\prime}\hat{H}_{\textrm{int}}(\tau^{\prime})\hat{H}_{\textrm{int}}(\tau^{\prime\prime})+\dots\,,

(6.30)

where, for simplicity, we introduce the short-hand notation
\infty_{\pm}\equiv\infty(1\pm i\epsilon)
.
Each order in
\hat{H}_{\textrm{int}}
is an interaction vertex, and carries both a time integral and the space integral (enclosed in the definition of
\hat{H}_{\textrm{int}}
) which in Fourier space enforces momentum conservation.

It is crucial to correctly identify the interaction Hamiltonian. Before proceeding in this direction, let us clarify our notation. We expand the action in the form

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}=\int d^{3}\vec{x}dt\,\underbrace{\mathcal{L}[\zeta(\vec{x},t),\dot{\zeta}(\vec{x},t),\partial_{k}\zeta(\vec{x},t)]}_{\equiv\,\mathcal{L}[\zeta(\vec{x},t)]}=\underbrace{\int d^{3}\vec{x}dt\,\mathcal{L}_{2}(\vec{x},t)}_{\equiv\,\mathcal{S}_{2}}+\underbrace{\int d^{3}\vec{x}dt\,\mathcal{L}_{3}[\zeta(\vec{x},t)]}_{\equiv\,\mathcal{S}_{3}}+\underbrace{\int d^{3}\vec{x}dt\,\mathcal{L}_{4}[\zeta(\vec{x},t)]}_{\equiv\,\mathcal{S}_{4}}+\dots\,,

(6.31)

with
\mathcal{S}_{2}
defined in eq. (
6.6
).
We also define (as a function of conformal time)

\displaystyle H_{\textrm{int}}^{(k)}(\tau)\equiv\int d^{3}\vec{x}\,\mathcal{H}_{k}[\zeta(\vec{x},\tau)]\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \hat{H}_{\textrm{int}}^{(k)}(\tau)\equiv\int d^{3}\vec{x}\,\mathcal{H}_{k}[\hat{\zeta}_{I}(\vec{x},\tau)]\,.

(6.32)

At the cubic order, we simply have

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{3}[\zeta(\vec{x},\tau)]=-\mathcal{L}_{3}[\zeta(\vec{x},\tau)]\,.

(6.33)

At the quartic order, simply writing
\mathcal{H}_{4}=-\mathcal{L}_{4}
does not capture the correct result if the cubic Lagrangian features interactions that depend on the time derivative of
\zeta
since the latter modify the definition of the conjugate momentum.

Using, at the operator level, the notation introduced in eq. (
6.32
), we schematically write at the first order in the Dyson series expansion

\displaystyle\langle\hat{\zeta}

\displaystyle(\vec{x}_{1},\tau)\hat{\zeta}(\vec{x}_{2},\tau)\rangle_{1^{\textrm{st}}}=

\displaystyle\langle 0|\hat{\zeta}_{I}(\vec{x}_{1},\tau)\hat{\zeta}_{I}(\vec{x}_{2},\tau)\bigg[-i\int_{-\infty_{-}}^{\tau}d\tau^{\prime}\hat{H}^{(4)}_{\textrm{int}}(\tau^{\prime})\bigg]|0\rangle+\langle 0|\bigg[i\int_{-\infty_{+}}^{\tau}d\tau^{\prime}\hat{H}^{(4)}_{\textrm{int}}(\tau^{\prime})\bigg]\hat{\zeta}_{I}(\vec{x}_{1},\tau)\hat{\zeta}_{I}(\vec{x}_{2},\tau)|0\rangle\,.

(6.34)

At the first order, therefore, the first non-zero quantum correction involves the quartic Hamiltonian.
At the second order in the Dyson series expansion and considering again terms with up to eight fields in the vacuum expectation values, we write schematically

\displaystyle\langle\hat{\zeta}(\vec{x}_{1},\tau)\hat{\zeta}(\vec{x}_{2},\tau)\rangle_{2^{\textrm{nd}}}

\displaystyle=\langle 0|\hat{\zeta}_{I}(\vec{x}_{1},\tau)\hat{\zeta}_{I}(\vec{x}_{2},\tau)\bigg[-\int_{-\infty_{-}}^{\tau}d\tau^{\prime}\int_{-\infty_{-}}^{\tau^{\prime}}d\tau^{\prime\prime}\hat{H}^{(3)}_{\textrm{int}}(\tau^{\prime})\hat{H}^{(3)}_{\textrm{int}}(\tau^{\prime\prime})\bigg]|0\rangle

\displaystyle+\langle 0|\bigg[-\int_{-\infty_{+}}^{\tau}d\tau^{\prime}\int_{-\infty_{+}}^{\tau^{\prime}}d\tau^{\prime\prime}\hat{H}^{(3)}_{\textrm{int}}(\tau^{\prime\prime})\hat{H}^{(3)}_{\textrm{int}}(\tau^{\prime})\bigg]\hat{\zeta}_{I}(\vec{x}_{1},\tau)\hat{\zeta}_{I}(\vec{x}_{2},\tau)|0\rangle

\displaystyle+\langle 0|\bigg[i\int_{-\infty_{+}}^{\tau}d\tau^{\prime}\hat{H}^{(3)}_{\textrm{int}}(\tau^{\prime})\bigg]\hat{\zeta}_{I}(\vec{x}_{1},\tau)\hat{\zeta}_{I}(\vec{x}_{2},\tau)\bigg[-i\int_{-\infty_{-}}^{\tau}d\tau^{\prime\prime}\hat{H}^{(3)}_{\textrm{int}}(\tau^{\prime\prime})\bigg]|0\rangle\,.

(6.35)

The vacuum expectation values of interacting-picture fields can be computed using Wick’s theorem.
Schematically, eqs. (
6.34
,
6.35
)
give rise to the following
connected diagrams.

\tau

(\vec{x}_{1},\tau)

(\vec{x}_{2},\tau)

\tau_{1}

\hat{H}^{(4)}_{\textrm{int}}(\tau_{1})

\tau

(\vec{x}_{1},\tau)

(\vec{x}_{2},\tau)

\tau_{1}

\tau_{2}

\hat{H}^{(3)}_{\textrm{int}}(\tau_{1})

\hat{H}^{(3)}_{\textrm{int}}(\tau_{2})

\tau

(\vec{x}_{1},\tau)

(\vec{x}_{2},\tau)

\tau_{1}

\tau_{2}

\hat{H}^{(3)}_{\textrm{int}}(\tau_{1})

\hat{H}^{(3)}_{\textrm{int}}(\tau_{2})

From the above classification, we see that, at the same loop order, we have three classes of connected diagrams that, in principle, should be discussed together. Notice that, contrary to the first two, the last diagram is not of 1-Particle-Irreducible (1PI) type since it consists of a tadpole attached to a two-point propagator. To proceed further, we need to specify the background dynamics, that shape the time evolution of the Hubble parameters, and the interaction Hamiltonian, that specifies which terms in the Dyson expansion contribute at a given perturbative order.

Cubic order

At the cubic order in the fluctuations,
the action is

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{3}

\displaystyle=\int d^{4}x\bigg\{\epsilon^{2}a^{3}\dot{\zeta}^{2}\zeta+\epsilon^{2}a\zeta(\partial_{k}\zeta)(\partial^{k}\zeta)-2\epsilon^{2}a^{3}\dot{\zeta}(\partial_{k}\zeta)\partial^{k}(\partial^{-2}\dot{\zeta})+\frac{\epsilon\dot{\epsilon_{2}}}{2}a^{3}\dot{\zeta}\zeta^{2}

\displaystyle-\frac{a^{3}\epsilon^{3}}{2}\big[\dot{\zeta}^{2}\zeta-\zeta\partial_{k}\partial_{l}(\partial^{-2}\dot{\zeta})\partial^{k}\partial^{l}(\partial^{-2}\dot{\zeta})\big]+\bigg[\frac{d}{dt}\left(\epsilon a^{3}\dot{\zeta}\right)-\epsilon a\partial_{k}\partial^{k}\zeta\bigg]\times

\displaystyle+\bigg[\frac{\epsilon_{2}\zeta^{2}}{2}+\frac{2\dot{\zeta}\zeta}{H}-\frac{(\partial_{k}\zeta)(\partial^{k}\zeta)}{2a^{2}H^{2}}+\frac{1}{2a^{2}H^{2}}\partial^{-2}\partial_{k}\partial_{l}(\partial^{k}\zeta\partial^{l}\zeta)\frac{\epsilon}{H}(\partial_{k}\zeta)\partial^{k}(\partial^{-2}\dot{\zeta})-\frac{\epsilon}{H}\partial^{-2}\partial_{k}\partial_{l}\partial^{k}\zeta\partial^{l}(\partial^{-2}\dot{\zeta})\bigg]\bigg\}\,.

(6.36)

As shown in ref.
[
684
]
, it is possible to simplify the cubic action by means of a field redefinition that introduces a non-linear shift in the original field.
Concretely, if we define

\displaystyle\zeta\equiv\zeta_{n}+f(\zeta_{n})\,,

(6.37)

with

\displaystyle f(

\displaystyle\zeta)\equiv\frac{1}{2}\bigg[\frac{\epsilon_{2}\zeta^{2}}{2}+\frac{2\dot{\zeta}\zeta}{H}-\frac{(\partial_{k}\zeta)(\partial^{k}\zeta)}{2a^{2}H^{2}}+\frac{1}{2a^{2}H^{2}}\partial^{-2}\partial_{k}\partial_{l}(\partial^{k}\zeta\partial^{l}\zeta)+\frac{\epsilon}{H}(\partial_{k}\zeta)\partial^{k}(\partial^{-2}\dot{\zeta})-\frac{\epsilon}{H}\partial^{-2}\partial_{k}\partial_{l}\partial^{k}\zeta\partial^{l}(\partial^{-2}\dot{\zeta})\bigg]\,,

(6.38)

we find, by direct computation, that at the quadratic order the field

\zeta_{n}
is described by the action

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{2}(\zeta_{n})=\int d^{4}x\,\epsilon\,a^{3}\left[\dot{\zeta}_{n}^{2}-\frac{(\partial_{k}\zeta_{n})(\partial^{k}\zeta_{n})}{a^{2}}\right]\,,

(6.39)

which has the same structure as the quadratic action for the original variable
\zeta
.
However, at the cubic order, we find

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{3}(\zeta_{n})=\int d^{4}x\bigg\{

\displaystyle\epsilon^{2}a^{3}\dot{\zeta}_{n}^{2}\zeta_{n}+\epsilon^{2}a\zeta_{n}(\partial_{k}\zeta_{n})(\partial^{k}\zeta_{n})-2\epsilon^{2}a^{3}\dot{\zeta}_{n}(\partial_{k}\zeta_{n})\partial^{k}(\partial^{-2}\dot{\zeta}_{n})+\frac{\epsilon\dot{\epsilon_{2}}}{2}a^{3}\dot{\zeta}_{n}\zeta_{n}^{2}

\displaystyle-\frac{a^{3}\epsilon^{3}}{2}\big[\dot{\zeta}_{n}^{2}\zeta_{n}-\zeta_{n}\partial_{k}\partial_{l}(\partial^{-2}\dot{\zeta}_{n})\partial^{k}\partial^{l}(\partial^{-2}\dot{\zeta}_{n})\big]\bigg\}\,,

(6.40)

in which, thanks to the above field redefinition, the second and third lines in the initial cubic action cancel out.

If we neglect terms with spatial derivatives and interactions suppressed by two or more powers of the Hubble parameter
\epsilon
, we find

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{3}(\zeta_{n})\ni\int d^{4}x\,\frac{\epsilon\dot{\epsilon_{2}}}{2}a^{3}\dot{\zeta}_{n}\zeta_{n}^{2}\,.

(6.41)

Notice that we do not count the coupling
\epsilon_{2}
as a slow-roll suppression since we are interested in the USR phase during which
|\epsilon_{2}|>3
and
\dot{\epsilon}_{2}\neq 0
.
Eq. (
6.41
) is the only interaction included in
ref.
[
683
]
.
This means that, implicitly, ref.
[
683
]
computes the two-point function for the field
\zeta_{n}
.
This is because, in terms of the dynamical variable
\zeta
, there is another interaction of order
\epsilon\epsilon_{2}
that should be included.

However, as stressed in ref.
[
684
]
,
\zeta_{n}
is not the right dynamical variable to consider since it is not conserved outside the horizon.
This is a trivial consequence of eq. (
6.37
).
Since
\zeta
is conserved outside the horizon,
\zeta_{n}
can not be conserved simply because various coefficients in the non-linear relation in eq. (
6.37
) are time-dependent.
Alternatively, as discussed in ref.
[
684
]
,
the above fact is also evident from the very same structure of the interactions that appear in eq. (
6.40
).
The interaction
\epsilon\dot{\epsilon}_{2}\dot{\zeta}_{n}\zeta_{n}^{2}
only has one time-derivative acting on the field
\zeta_{n}
; consequently,
it alters
the value of
\zeta_{n}
on super-horizon scales (if one computes the equation of motion for
\zeta_{n}
, it is easy to see that the constant solution is not stable).
Let us make the above considerations more concrete.
Eventually, we are interested in the computation of the two-point function for the original curvature field.
Given the field redefinition in eq. (
6.37
), we write

\displaystyle\langle\hat{\zeta}(\vec{x}_{1},\tau)\hat{\zeta}(\vec{x}_{2},\tau)\rangle=

\displaystyle\langle\big\{\hat{\zeta}_{n}(\vec{x}_{1},\tau)+f[\hat{\zeta}_{n}(\vec{x}_{1},\tau)]\big\}\big\{\hat{\zeta}_{n}(\vec{x}_{2},\tau)+f[\hat{\zeta}_{n}(\vec{x}_{2},\tau)]\big\}\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle\hat{\zeta}_{n}(\vec{x}_{1},\tau)\hat{\zeta}_{n}(\vec{x}_{2},\tau)\rangle+

(6.42)

\displaystyle\langle\hat{\zeta}_{n}(\vec{x}_{1},\tau)f[\hat{\zeta}_{n}(\vec{x}_{2},\tau)]\rangle+\langle f[\hat{\zeta}_{n}(\vec{x}_{1},\tau)]\hat{\zeta}_{n}(\vec{x}_{1},\tau)\rangle+

(6.43)

\displaystyle\langle f[\hat{\zeta}_{n}(\vec{x}_{1},\tau)]f[\hat{\zeta}_{n}(\vec{x}_{2},\tau)]\rangle\,,

(6.44)

The first term, eq. (
6.42
), corresponds to the two-point function for the shifted curvature field whose cubic action is given by eq. (
6.40
);

\langle\hat{\zeta}_{n}(\vec{x}_{1},\tau)\hat{\zeta}_{n}(\vec{x}_{2},\tau)\rangle
can be computed perturbatively by means of
the “
in
-
in
” formalism.
Eqs. (
6.43
,
6.44
) account for the difference between
\zeta
and
\zeta_{n}
at the non-linear level.
Notice that the first term in the
functional form in eq. (
6.38
) does not die off in the late-time limit
\tau\to 0^{-}
(in which the power spectrum must be eventually evaluated)
if we consider the case in which

\epsilon_{2}\neq 0
after the USR phase (as expected in realistic single-field models.
However, if we limit to the case in which

\eta_{\rm III}=0
the contribution from the field redefinition vanishes.
This limit was considered in
ref.
[
683
]
.
In order to make contact with the analysis presented in ref.
[
683
]
, we shall also adopt in the bulk of this analysis the assumption
\eta_{\rm III}=0
.

Let us now come back to the schematic in eq. (
6.2.1
).
The cubic Hamiltonian interaction that follows from eq. (
6.41
) gives rise to the last two topologies of connected diagrams illustrated in eq. (
6.2.1
).
As in ref.
[
683
]
, we will only focus on the 1PI diagram, that is, the central diagram in eq. (
6.2.1
).
The last diagram in eq. (
6.2.1
) consists of a tadpole
that is attached to a
\zeta
-propagator and affects at one-loop its two-point correlation function.
The correct way to deal with tadpoles is by changing the background solution, cf. ref.
[
700
]
for a discussion in the case of ordinary SR inflation and ref.
[
685
]
for the case in which there are additional spectator fields. Recently, ref.
[
701
]
estimated the tadpole correction to the background evolution in the context of a model in which there is a resonant amplification of field fluctuations.
Imposing the condition that such modification is negligible could give rise to an additional perturbativity bound. In this analysis, for simplicity, we neglect the contribution of such tadpole.

Beyond the cubic action

Before proceeding, we comment about quartic interactions since, as qualitatively discussed in eq. (
6.2.1
), they give rise to one-loop corrections which are of the same order if compared to those generated by cubic interaction terms.
The derivation of the fourth-order action has been discussed in ref.
[
702
]
.
Based on this result, ref.
[
683
,
689
]
claims that the relevant quartic interaction in the case of USR dynamics (that is, the quartic interaction proportional to
\dot{\epsilon_{2}}
) gives a vanishing contribution when inserted in eq. (
6.34
).
Ref.
[
691
]
adopts an approach based on the effective field theory of inflation and includes cubic and quartic interactions. It finds that the latter gives a non-trivial contribution, and finds a loop-corrected power spectrum different from the one in ref.
[
683
,
689
]
. It would be important to perform a consistent comparison between these two approaches, including the full cubic and quartic interactions in both cases.
Generally speaking, we expect cubic and quartic interactions to be inextricably linked
[
703
,
704
]
.
For instance, the quartic Hamiltonian receives a
contribution that arises from the modification of the conjugate momentum if there are cubic interactions which depend on
\dot{\zeta}
.
Similarly, cubic interactions with spatial derivatives are paired with quartic interactions induced by a residual spatial conformal symmetry of the perturbed metric
[
685
]
.
En route, we notice that interactions with spatial derivatives are usually neglected for modes that are super-horizon. However, in the spirit of the loop computation in ref.
[
683
]
, the momenta over which the loop is integrated cross the horizon during the USR phase, and, naïvely, their spatial derivatives do not pay any super-horizon suppression.

In this section, as a preliminary step towards a more complete analysis and in order to compare our results with the claim made in refs.
[
688
,
689
,
690
,
691
,
692
,
693
,
694
,
695
,
696
]
,
we only focus on the cubic interaction in eq. (
6.41
).
However, we stress that
all the arguments listed above motivate the need for a more comprehensive analysis.

6.2.2
One-loop computation

We consider in this section the cubic interaction Hamiltonian given by (we omit the subscript
I
in the interaction-picture fields)

\displaystyle\hat{H}_{\rm int}^{(3)}(\tau)=\frac{1}{2}\int d^{3}\vec{x}\,\epsilon(\tau)\epsilon_{2}^{\prime}(\tau)a^{2}(\tau)\zeta^{\prime}(\vec{x},\tau)\zeta(\vec{x},\tau)^{2}\,.

(6.45)

We consider
eq. (
6.35
);
this can be written in the compact form

\displaystyle\langle\hat{\zeta}(\vec{x}_{1},\tau)

\displaystyle\hat{\zeta}(\vec{x}_{2},\tau)\rangle_{2^{\rm nd}}=\langle\hat{\zeta}(\vec{x}_{1},\tau)\hat{\zeta}(\vec{x}_{2},\tau)\rangle_{2^{{\rm nd}}}^{(1,1)}-2{\rm Re}\left[\langle\hat{\zeta}(\vec{x}_{1},\tau)\hat{\zeta}(\vec{x}_{2},\tau)\rangle_{2^{{\rm{nd}}}}^{(0,2)}\right]

(6.46)

where

\displaystyle\langle\hat{\zeta}(\vec{x}_{1},\tau)\hat{\zeta}(\vec{x}_{2},\tau)\rangle_{2^{{\rm{nd}}}}^{(1,1)}\equiv

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty(1+i\epsilon)}^{\tau}d\tau_{1}\int_{-\infty(1-i\epsilon)}^{\tau}d\tau_{2}\langle 0|\hat{H}_{{\rm int}}(\tau_{1})\hat{\zeta}_{I}(\vec{x}_{1},\tau)\hat{\zeta}_{I}(\vec{x}_{2},\tau)\hat{H}_{{\rm{int}}}(\tau_{2})|0\rangle\,,

(6.47)

\displaystyle\langle\hat{\zeta}(\vec{x}_{1},\tau)\hat{\zeta}(\vec{x}_{2},\tau)\rangle_{2^{\textrm{nd}}}^{(0,2)}\equiv

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty(1-i\epsilon)}^{\tau}d\tau_{1}\int_{-\infty(1-i\epsilon)}^{\tau_{1}}d\tau_{2}\langle 0|\hat{\zeta}_{I}(\vec{x}_{1},\tau)\hat{\zeta}_{I}(\vec{x}_{2},\tau)\hat{H}_{\textrm{int}}(\tau_{1})\hat{H}_{\textrm{int}}(\tau_{2})|0\rangle\,.

(6.48)

This expansion is consistent with Eq. (16) of Ref.
[
685
]
.
Consider the first contribution in eq. (
6.47
), one finds

\displaystyle\langle\hat{\zeta}(\vec{x}_{1},\tau)\hat{\zeta}(\vec{x}_{2},\tau)\rangle_{2^{\textrm{nd}}}^{(1,1)}=\frac{1}{4}

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty_{+}}^{\tau}d\tau_{1}\epsilon(\tau_{1})\epsilon_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})a^{2}(\tau_{1})\int_{-\infty_{-}}^{\tau}d\tau_{2}\epsilon(\tau_{2})\epsilon_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{2})a^{2}(\tau_{2})\int d^{3}\vec{y}d^{3}\vec{z}

\displaystyle\int\left[\prod_{i=1}^{8}\frac{d^{3}\vec{k}_{i}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\right]e^{i\vec{y}\cdot(\vec{k}_{1}+\vec{k}_{2}+\vec{k}_{3})}e^{i(\vec{x}_{1}\cdot\vec{k}_{4}+\vec{x}_{2}\cdot\vec{k}_{5})}e^{i\vec{z}\cdot(\vec{k}_{6}+\vec{k}_{7}+\vec{k}_{8})}

\displaystyle\langle 0|\hat{\zeta}^{\prime}_{I}(\vec{k}_{1},\tau_{1})\hat{\zeta}_{I}(\vec{k}_{2},\tau_{1})\hat{\zeta}_{I}(\vec{k}_{3},\tau_{1})\hat{\zeta}_{I}(\vec{k}_{4},\tau)\hat{\zeta}_{I}(\vec{k}_{5},\tau)\hat{\zeta}^{\prime}_{I}(\vec{k}_{6},\tau_{2})\hat{\zeta}_{I}(\vec{k}_{7},\tau_{2})\hat{\zeta}_{I}(\vec{k}_{8},\tau_{2})|0\rangle\,.

(6.49)

The
36
connected Wick contractions can be expressed as

\displaystyle\langle\hat{\zeta}(\vec{x}_{1},\tau)\hat{\zeta}(\vec{x}_{2},\tau)\rangle_{2^{{\rm nd}}}^{(1,1)}=

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty_{+}}^{\tau}d\tau_{1}\epsilon(\tau_{1})\epsilon_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})a^{2}(\tau_{1})\int_{-\infty_{-}}^{\tau}d\tau_{2}\epsilon(\tau_{2})\epsilon_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{2})a^{2}(\tau_{2})\int\frac{d^{3}\vec{k}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\frac{d^{3}\vec{q}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\,e^{i(\vec{x}_{1}-\vec{x}_{2})\cdot(\vec{k}+\vec{q})}

\displaystyle\big[\left|\zeta_{k+q}(\tau)\right|^{2}\big\{\zeta_{k}(\tau_{1})\zeta^{\prime}_{k+q}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{q}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{k}^{*}(\tau_{2})\zeta_{k+q}^{\prime\,*}(\tau_{2})\zeta_{q}^{*}(\tau_{2})+

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\zeta_{k}(\tau_{1})\zeta^{\prime}_{k+q}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{q}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{k+q}^{*}(\tau_{2})\big[\zeta_{k}^{\prime\,*}(\tau_{2})\zeta_{q}^{*}(\tau_{2})+\zeta_{k}^{*}(\tau_{2})\zeta_{q}^{\prime\,*}(\tau_{2})\big]+

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\zeta_{k}^{*}(\tau_{2})\zeta^{\prime\,*}_{k+q}(\tau_{2})\zeta_{q}^{*}(\tau_{2})\zeta_{k+q}(\tau_{1})\big[\zeta_{k}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{q}(\tau_{1})+\zeta_{k}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{q}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})\big]+

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\zeta_{k}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{k+q}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{q}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{k+q}^{*}(\tau_{2})\big[\zeta_{q}^{*}(\tau_{2})\zeta_{k}^{\prime\,*}(\tau_{2})+\zeta_{k}^{*}(\tau_{2})\zeta_{q}^{\prime\,*}(\tau_{2})\big]+

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\zeta_{k}^{\prime\,*}(\tau_{2})\zeta_{k+q}^{*}(\tau_{2})\zeta_{q}^{*}(\tau_{2})\zeta_{k+q}(\tau_{1})\big[\zeta_{q}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{k}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})+\zeta_{k}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{q}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})\big]\big\}\big]\,.

(6.50)

Consider now eq. (
6.48
).
One can write it in the form

\displaystyle\langle\hat{\zeta}(\vec{x}_{1},\tau)\hat{\zeta}(\vec{x}_{2},\tau)\rangle_{2^{\textrm{nd}}}^{(0,2)}=\frac{1}{4}

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty_{-}}^{\tau}d\tau_{1}\epsilon(\tau_{1})\epsilon_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})a^{2}(\tau_{1})\int_{-\infty_{-}}^{\tau_{1}}d\tau_{2}\epsilon(\tau_{2})\epsilon_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{2})a^{2}(\tau_{2})\int d^{3}\vec{y}d^{3}\vec{z}

\displaystyle\int\left[\prod_{i=1}^{8}\frac{d^{3}\vec{k}_{i}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\right]e^{i\vec{y}\cdot(\vec{k}_{1}+\vec{k}_{2}+\vec{k}_{3})}e^{i(\vec{x}_{1}\cdot\vec{k}_{4}+\vec{x}_{2}\cdot\vec{k}_{5})}e^{i\vec{z}\cdot(\vec{k}_{6}+\vec{k}_{7}+\vec{k}_{8})}

\displaystyle\langle 0|\hat{\zeta}_{I}(\vec{k}_{4},\tau)\hat{\zeta}_{I}(\vec{k}_{5},\tau)\hat{\zeta}^{\prime}_{I}(\vec{k}_{1},\tau_{1})\hat{\zeta}_{I}(\vec{k}_{2},\tau_{1})\hat{\zeta}_{I}(\vec{k}_{3},\tau_{1})\hat{\zeta}^{\prime}_{I}(\vec{k}_{6},\tau_{2})\hat{\zeta}_{I}(\vec{k}_{7},\tau_{2})\hat{\zeta}_{I}(\vec{k}_{8},\tau_{2})|0\rangle\,.

(6.51)

After Wick contractions, we find

\displaystyle\langle\hat{\zeta}(\vec{x}_{1},\tau)\hat{\zeta}(\vec{x}_{2},\tau)\rangle_{2^{\textrm{nd}}}^{(0,2)}=

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty_{-}}^{\tau}d\tau_{1}\epsilon(\tau_{1})\epsilon_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})a^{2}(\tau_{1})\int_{-\infty_{-}}^{\tau_{1}}d\tau_{2}\epsilon(\tau_{2})\epsilon_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{2})a^{2}(\tau_{2})\int\frac{d^{3}\vec{k}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\frac{d^{3}\vec{q}}{(2\pi)^{3}}e^{i(\vec{x}_{1}-\vec{x}_{2})\cdot(\vec{k}+\vec{q})}

\displaystyle\big[\zeta_{k+q}^{2}(\tau)\big\{\zeta_{k}(\tau_{1})\zeta^{\prime\,*}_{k+q}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{q}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{k}^{*}(\tau_{2})\zeta_{k+q}^{\prime\,*}(\tau_{2})\zeta_{q}^{*}(\tau_{2})+

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912pt\zeta_{k}(\tau_{1})\zeta^{\prime\,*}_{k+q}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{q}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{k+q}^{*}(\tau_{2})\big[\zeta_{k}^{\prime\,*}(\tau_{2})\zeta_{q}^{*}(\tau_{2})+\zeta_{k}^{*}(\tau_{2})\zeta_{q}^{\prime\,*}(\tau_{2})\big]+

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912pt\zeta_{k}^{*}(\tau_{2})\zeta^{\prime\,*}_{k+q}(\tau_{2})\zeta_{q}^{*}(\tau_{2})\zeta_{k+q}^{*}(\tau_{1})\big[\zeta_{k}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{q}(\tau_{1})+\zeta_{k}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{q}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})\big]+

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912pt\zeta_{k}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{k+q}^{*}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{q}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{k+q}^{*}(\tau_{2})\big[\zeta_{q}^{*}(\tau_{2})\zeta_{k}^{\prime\,*}(\tau_{2})+\zeta_{k}^{*}(\tau_{2})\zeta_{q}^{\prime\,*}(\tau_{2})\big]+

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912pt\zeta_{k}^{\prime\,*}(\tau_{2})\zeta_{k+q}^{*}(\tau_{2})\zeta_{q}^{*}(\tau_{2})\zeta_{k+q}^{*}(\tau_{1})\big[\zeta_{q}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{k}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})+\zeta_{k}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{q}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})\big]\big\}\big]\,.

(6.52)

At this point we shift the momentum following the prescription
k\rightarrow k-q
in such a way that
k
is identified with the external momentum.
The power spectrum at one loop can be therefore written as

\mathcal{P}(k)=\lim_{\tau\to 0^{-}}\left(\frac{k^{3}}{2\pi^{2}}\right)\left\{\left|\zeta_{k}(\tau)\right|^{2}+\frac{1}{(4\pi)^{2}}\left[\Delta P_{1}(k,\tau)+\Delta P_{2}(k,\tau)\right]\right\}\,,

(6.53)

with

\displaystyle\Delta P_{1}(k,\tau)

\displaystyle\equiv 4\int_{-\infty_{+}}^{\tau}d\tau_{1}\epsilon(\tau_{1})\epsilon_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})a^{2}(\tau_{1})\int_{-\infty_{-}}^{\tau}d\tau_{2}\epsilon(\tau_{2})\epsilon_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{2})a^{2}(\tau_{2})\int_{0}^{\infty}dq\,q^{2}\,d(\cos\theta)\left|\zeta_{k}(\tau)\right|^{2}

\displaystyle\times\big\{\zeta_{k-q}(\tau_{1})\zeta^{\prime}_{k}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{q}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{k-q}^{*}(\tau_{2})\zeta_{k}^{\prime\,*}(\tau_{2})\zeta_{q}^{*}(\tau_{2})+

\displaystyle\hskip 17.64056pt\zeta_{k-q}(\tau_{1})\zeta^{\prime}_{k}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{q}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{k}^{*}(\tau_{2})\big[\zeta_{k-q}^{\prime\,*}(\tau_{2})\zeta_{q}^{*}(\tau_{2})+\zeta_{k-q}^{*}(\tau_{2})\zeta_{q}^{\prime\,*}(\tau_{2})\big]+

\displaystyle\hskip 17.64056pt\zeta_{k-q}^{*}(\tau_{2})\zeta^{\prime\,*}_{k}(\tau_{2})\zeta_{q}^{*}(\tau_{2})\zeta_{k}(\tau_{1})\big[\zeta_{k-q}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{q}(\tau_{1})+\zeta_{k-q}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{q}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})\big]+

\displaystyle\hskip 17.64056pt\zeta_{k-q}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{k}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{q}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{k}^{*}(\tau_{2})\big[\zeta_{q}^{*}(\tau_{2})\zeta_{k-q}^{\prime\,*}(\tau_{2})+\zeta_{k-q}^{*}(\tau_{2})\zeta_{q}^{\prime\,*}(\tau_{2})\big]+

\displaystyle\hskip 17.64056pt\zeta_{k-q}^{\prime\,*}(\tau_{2})\zeta_{k}^{*}(\tau_{2})\zeta_{q}^{*}(\tau_{2})\zeta_{k}(\tau_{1})\big[\zeta_{q}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{k-q}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})+\zeta_{k-q}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{q}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})\big]\big\}\,,

(6.54)

\displaystyle\Delta P_{2}(k,\tau)

\displaystyle\equiv-8{\rm Re}\Big[\int_{-\infty_{-}}^{\tau}d\tau_{1}\epsilon(\tau_{1})\epsilon_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})a^{2}(\tau_{1})\int_{-\infty_{-}}^{\tau_{1}}d\tau_{2}\epsilon(\tau_{2})\epsilon_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{2})a^{2}(\tau_{2})\int_{0}^{\infty}dq\,q^{2}\,d(\cos\theta)\zeta_{k}^{2}(\tau)

\displaystyle\times\big\{\zeta_{k-q}(\tau_{1})\zeta^{\prime\,*}_{k}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{q}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{k-q}^{*}(\tau_{2})\zeta_{k}^{\prime\,*}(\tau_{2})\zeta_{q}^{*}(\tau_{2})+

\displaystyle\hskip 17.64056pt\zeta_{k-q}(\tau_{1})\zeta^{\prime\,*}_{k}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{q}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{k}^{*}(\tau_{2})\big[\zeta_{k-q}^{\prime\,*}(\tau_{2})\zeta_{q}^{*}(\tau_{2})+\zeta_{k-q}^{*}(\tau_{2})\zeta_{q}^{\prime\,*}(\tau_{2})\big]+

\displaystyle\hskip 17.64056pt\zeta_{k-q}^{*}(\tau_{2})\zeta^{\prime\,*}_{k}(\tau_{2})\zeta_{q}^{*}(\tau_{2})\zeta_{k}^{*}(\tau_{1})\big[\zeta_{k-q}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{q}(\tau_{1})+\zeta_{k-q}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{q}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})\big]+

\displaystyle\hskip 17.64056pt\zeta_{k-q}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{k}^{*}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{q}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{k}^{*}(\tau_{2})\big[\zeta_{q}^{*}(\tau_{2})\zeta_{k-q}^{\prime\,*}(\tau_{2})+\zeta_{k-q}^{*}(\tau_{2})\zeta_{q}^{\prime\,*}(\tau_{2})\big]+

\displaystyle\hskip 17.64056pt\zeta_{k-q}^{\prime\,*}(\tau_{2})\zeta_{k}^{*}(\tau_{2})\zeta_{q}^{*}(\tau_{2})\zeta_{k}^{*}(\tau_{1})\big[\zeta_{q}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{k-q}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})+\zeta_{k-q}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{q}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})\big]\big\}\Big]\,.

(6.55)

Loop correction with a large hierarchy of scales

First, we will be concerned with external momenta that describe the large CMB scales, while the USR takes place when modes
k_{\rm USR}\gg k
cross the horizon.
The situation is summarized in the following schematic

\textrm{comoving length}\,\lambda

comoving time

\textrm{long mode}\,\lambda=1/k

short modes

q_{\textrm{in}}

q_{\textrm{end}}

\tau

1/aH

\tau\to-\infty

\tau_{k}

\tau\to 0^{-}

USR

\tau_{\textrm{in}}

\tau_{\textrm{end}}

(6.56)

in which the blue horizontal band represents the interval of modes that cross the horizon during the USR phase, the vertical band shaded in magenta.
In other words, as we will restrict the integration over momenta
q\in[q_{\rm in},q_{\rm end}]
that are enhanced by the USR phase, we can assume
q\gg k
.
Consequently, as in ref.
[
683
]
,
we approximate

\displaystyle k-q=\sqrt{k^{2}+q^{2}-2kq\cos(\theta)}\approx q\,,\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \int_{-1}^{+1}d(\cos\theta)=2\,.

(6.57)

With these assumptions, we can further simplify the expressions.
We collect each contribution depending on the number of time derivatives acting on the long mode
\zeta_{k}
.
In each expression, the first line indicates terms with no derivative on the long modes, the second one those with one derivative, while the last with two.
One finds

\displaystyle\Delta P_{1}(k,\tau)

\displaystyle\equiv 8\int_{\tau_{\rm in}}^{\tau}d\tau_{1}\epsilon(\tau_{1})\epsilon_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})a^{2}(\tau_{1})\int_{\tau_{\rm in}}^{\tau}d\tau_{2}\epsilon(\tau_{2})\epsilon_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{2})a^{2}(\tau_{2})\int_{q_{\rm in}}^{q_{\rm end}}dq\,q^{2}\,\left|\zeta_{k}(\tau)\right|^{2}

\displaystyle\times\big\{4\zeta_{k}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{k}^{*}(\tau_{2})\zeta_{q}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{q}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{q}^{*}(\tau_{2})\zeta_{q}^{\prime\,*}(\tau_{2})+

\displaystyle\hskip 17.64056pt2\zeta^{\prime}_{k}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{k}^{*}(\tau_{2})\zeta_{q}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{q}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{q}^{\prime\,*}(\tau_{2})\zeta_{q}^{*}(\tau_{2})+2\zeta_{k}(\tau_{1})\zeta^{\prime\,*}_{k}(\tau_{2})\zeta_{q}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{q}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{q}^{*}(\tau_{2})\zeta_{q}^{*}(\tau_{2})+

\displaystyle\hskip 17.64056pt\zeta^{\prime}_{k}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{k}^{\prime\,*}(\tau_{2})\zeta_{q}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{q}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{q}^{*}(\tau_{2})\zeta_{q}^{*}(\tau_{2})\big\}\,,

(6.58)

\displaystyle\Delta P_{2}(k,\tau)

\displaystyle\equiv-16{\rm Re}\Big[\int_{\tau_{\rm in}}^{\tau}d\tau_{1}\epsilon(\tau_{1})\epsilon_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})a^{2}(\tau_{1})\int_{\tau_{\rm in}}^{\tau_{1}}d\tau_{2}\epsilon(\tau_{2})\epsilon_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{2})a^{2}(\tau_{2})\int_{q_{\rm in}}^{q_{\rm end}}dq\,q^{2}\,\zeta_{k}(\tau)^{2}

\displaystyle\times\big\{4\zeta_{k}^{*}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{k}^{*}(\tau_{2})\zeta_{q}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{q}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{q}^{*}(\tau_{2})\zeta_{q}^{\prime\,*}(\tau_{2})+

\displaystyle\hskip 17.64056pt2\zeta^{\prime\,*}_{k}(\tau_{1})\zeta^{*}_{k}(\tau_{2})\zeta_{q}(\tau_{1})^{2}\zeta_{q}^{\prime\,*}(\tau_{2})\zeta_{q}^{*}(\tau_{2})+2\zeta^{*}_{k}(\tau_{1})\zeta^{\prime\,*}_{k}(\tau_{2})\zeta_{q}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{q}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{q}^{*}(\tau_{2})^{2}+

\displaystyle\hskip 17.64056pt\zeta^{\prime\,*}_{k}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{k}^{\prime\,*}(\tau_{2})\zeta_{q}(\tau_{1})^{2}\zeta_{q}^{*}(\tau_{2})^{2}\big\}\Big]\,.

(6.59)

We can combine the two contributions using the properties of symmetric integrals for holomorphic symmetric functions

f(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})=f(\tau_{2},\tau_{1})

[
705
]

\int_{\tau_{\rm in}}^{\tau}d\tau_{1}\int_{\tau_{\rm in}}^{\tau_{1}}d\tau_{2}f\left(\tau_{1},\tau_{2}\right)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\tau_{\rm in}}^{\tau}d\tau_{1}\int_{\tau_{\rm in}}^{\tau}d\tau_{2}f\left(\tau_{1},\tau_{2}\right).

(6.60)

To shorten the notation, we introduce
\Delta P(k,\tau)=\Delta P_{1}(k,\tau)+\Delta P_{2}(k,\tau)
and collect the individual contribution order by order in derivatives:

•

0th order in time derivatives of the long mode.

For ease of reading, we introduce
the short-hand notation

\epsilon(\tau)\epsilon_{2}^{\prime}(\tau)a^{2}(\tau)\equiv g(\tau)
.
Consider the sum of the two integrals

\displaystyle\Delta P_{0{\rm th}}

\displaystyle(k,\tau)=32\int_{\tau_{\rm in}}^{\tau}d\tau_{1}g(\tau_{1})\int_{\tau_{\rm in}}^{\tau}d\tau_{2}g(\tau_{2})\int_{q_{\rm in}}^{q_{\rm end}}dq\,q^{2}\,\left|\zeta_{k}(\tau)\right|^{2}\zeta_{k}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{k}^{*}(\tau_{2})\zeta_{q}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{q}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{q}^{*}(\tau_{2})\zeta_{q}^{\prime\,*}(\tau_{2})

(6.61)

\displaystyle-64{\rm Re}\Big[\int_{\tau_{\rm in}}^{\tau}d\tau_{1}g(\tau_{1})\int_{\tau_{\rm in}}^{\tau_{1}}d\tau_{2}g(\tau_{2})\int_{q_{\rm in}}^{q_{\rm end}}dq\,q^{2}\,\zeta_{k}(\tau)^{2}\zeta_{k}^{*}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{k}^{*}(\tau_{2})\zeta_{q}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{q}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{q}^{*}(\tau_{2})\zeta_{q}^{\prime\,*}(\tau_{2})\Big]\,.

(6.62)

We notice that, in the first integral in eq. (
6.61
), the exchange
\tau_{1}\leftrightarrow\tau_{2}
transforms

\displaystyle\zeta_{k}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{k}^{*}(\tau_{2})\zeta_{q}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{q}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{q}^{*}(\tau_{2})\zeta_{q}^{\prime\,*}(\tau_{2})\overset{\tau_{1}\leftrightarrow\tau_{2}}{\Longrightarrow}

\displaystyle\zeta_{k}(\tau_{2})\zeta_{k}^{*}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{q}(\tau_{2})\zeta_{q}^{\prime}(\tau_{2})\zeta_{q}^{*}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{q}^{\prime\,*}(\tau_{1})=

\displaystyle[\zeta_{k}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{k}^{*}(\tau_{2})\zeta_{q}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{q}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{q}^{*}(\tau_{2})\zeta_{q}^{\prime\,*}(\tau_{2})]^{*}\,.

(6.63)

Therefore, the first integral in eq. (
6.61
) is fully symmetric under the exchange
\tau_{1}\leftrightarrow\tau_{2}
, and
we rewrite
\Delta P_{0{\rm th}}(k,\tau)
as

\displaystyle\Delta P_{0{\rm th}}

\displaystyle(k,\tau)=

\displaystyle 32\int_{\tau_{\rm in}}^{\tau}d\tau_{1}g(\tau_{1})\int_{\tau_{\rm in}}^{\tau}d\tau_{2}g(\tau_{2})\int_{q_{\rm in}}^{q_{\rm end}}dq\,q^{2}\,\left|\zeta_{k}(\tau)\right|^{2}{\rm Re}\big[\zeta_{k}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{k}^{*}(\tau_{2})\zeta_{q}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{q}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{q}^{*}(\tau_{2})\zeta_{q}^{\prime\,*}(\tau_{2})\big]

(6.64)

\displaystyle-64\int_{\tau_{\rm in}}^{\tau}d\tau_{1}g(\tau_{1})\int_{\tau_{\rm in}}^{\tau_{1}}d\tau_{2}g(\tau_{2})\int_{q_{\rm in}}^{q_{\rm end}}dq\,q^{2}\,{\rm Re}\big[\zeta_{k}(\tau)^{2}\zeta_{k}^{*}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{k}^{*}(\tau_{2})\zeta_{q}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{q}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{q}^{*}(\tau_{2})\zeta_{q}^{\prime\,*}(\tau_{2})\big]\,.

(6.65)

and apply to the first integral in eq. (
6.64
)
the identity in eq. (
6.60
).
We arrive at

\displaystyle\Delta P_{0{\rm th}}(k,\tau)

\displaystyle=64\int_{\tau_{\rm in}}^{\tau}d\tau_{1}g(\tau_{1})\int_{\tau_{\rm in}}^{\tau_{1}}d\tau_{2}g(\tau_{2})\int_{q_{\rm in}}^{q_{\rm end}}dq\,q^{2}\times

\displaystyle\{{\rm Re}\big[\zeta_{k}(\tau)\zeta_{k}^{*}(\tau)\zeta_{k}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{k}^{*}(\tau_{2})\zeta_{q}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{q}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{q}^{*}(\tau_{2})\zeta_{q}^{\prime\,*}(\tau_{2})\big]

\displaystyle-{\rm Re}\big[\zeta_{k}(\tau)^{2}\zeta_{k}^{*}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{k}^{*}(\tau_{2})\zeta_{q}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{q}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{q}^{*}(\tau_{2})\zeta_{q}^{\prime\,*}(\tau_{2})\big]\}\,.

(6.66)

We are now in the position of combining the two integrand functions.
Schematically, we define the two combinations

\displaystyle X\equiv\zeta_{k}^{*}(\tau)\zeta_{k}(\tau_{1})\,,\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ Y\equiv\zeta_{k}(\tau)\zeta_{q}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{q}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{k}^{*}(\tau_{2})\zeta_{q}^{*}(\tau_{2})\zeta_{q}^{\prime\,*}(\tau_{2})\,,

(6.67)

such that the integrand in eq. (
6.66
) becomes

\displaystyle{\rm Re}(XY)-{\rm Re}(X^{*}Y)=-2{\rm Im}(X){\rm Im}(Y)\,.

(6.68)

We thus arrive at the result

Contribution with no time derivatives on the long mode
k

\displaystyle\Delta P_{0{\rm th}}(k,\tau)=

\displaystyle-128\int_{\tau_{\rm in}}^{\tau}d\tau_{1}\epsilon(\tau_{1})\epsilon_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})a^{2}(\tau_{1})\int_{\tau_{\rm in}}^{\tau_{1}}d\tau_{2}\epsilon(\tau_{2})\epsilon_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{2})a^{2}(\tau_{2})\int_{q_{\rm in}}^{q_{\rm end}}dq\,q^{2}

\displaystyle\times{\rm Im}\left[\zeta_{k}^{*}(\tau)\zeta_{k}(\tau_{1})\right]{\rm Im}\left[\zeta_{k}(\tau)\zeta_{q}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{q}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{k}^{*}(\tau_{2})\zeta_{q}^{*}(\tau_{2})\zeta_{q}^{\prime\,*}(\tau_{2})\right].

(6.69)

Given that we are interested in modes
k
that are much smaller than the USR-enhanced ones, they are super-horizon at the time of USR phase. Thus, for any time
\tau\gtrsim\tau_{\rm in}
of relevance for both time integrations, one has that

{\rm Im}\left[\zeta_{k}(\tau)\zeta_{k}^{*}(\tau_{1})\right]\simeq{\rm Im}\left[|\zeta_{k}(\tau)|^{2}\right]=0,

(6.70)

which makes the above contribution negligible.

•

1st order in time derivatives of the long mode.

Starting from the second lines of eqs. (
6.58
,
6.59
), we now consider the sum

\displaystyle\Delta P_{\rm 1st}(k,\tau)=

\displaystyle 16\int_{\tau_{\rm in}}^{\tau}d\tau_{1}g(\tau_{1})\int_{\tau_{\rm in}}^{\tau}d\tau_{2}g(\tau_{2})\int_{q_{\rm in}}^{q_{\rm end}}dq\,q^{2}\,\left|\zeta_{k}(\tau)\right|^{2}

\displaystyle\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \big[\zeta^{\prime}_{k}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{k}^{*}(\tau_{2})\zeta_{q}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{q}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{q}^{\prime\,*}(\tau_{2})\zeta_{q}^{*}(\tau_{2})+\zeta_{k}(\tau_{1})\zeta^{\prime\,*}_{k}(\tau_{2})\zeta_{q}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{q}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{q}^{*}(\tau_{2})\zeta_{q}^{*}(\tau_{2})\big]

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle 32{\rm Re}\Big\{\int_{\tau_{\rm in}}^{\tau}d\tau_{1}g(\tau_{1})\int_{\tau_{\rm in}}^{\tau_{1}}d\tau_{2}g(\tau_{2})\int_{q_{\rm in}}^{q_{\rm end}}dq\,q^{2}\zeta_{k}(\tau)^{2}

\displaystyle\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \big[\zeta^{\prime\,*}_{k}(\tau_{1})\zeta^{*}_{k}(\tau_{2})\zeta_{q}(\tau_{1})^{2}\zeta_{q}^{\prime\,*}(\tau_{2})\zeta_{q}^{*}(\tau_{2})+\zeta^{*}_{k}(\tau_{1})\zeta^{\prime\,*}_{k}(\tau_{2})\zeta_{q}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{q}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{q}^{*}(\tau_{2})^{2}\big]\Big\}\,.

(6.71)

Manipulations analogue to those discussed in the previous point allow one to combine the two integrals together. We find

\displaystyle\Delta P_{\rm 1st}(k,\tau)=

\displaystyle-64\int_{\tau_{\rm in}}^{\tau}d\tau_{1}g(\tau_{1})\int_{\tau_{\rm in}}^{\tau_{1}}d\tau_{2}g(\tau_{2})\int_{q_{\rm in}}^{q_{\rm end}}dq\,q^{2}

\displaystyle\{{\rm Im}\left[\zeta_{k}^{*}(\tau)\zeta_{k}(\tau_{1})\right]{\rm Im}\left[\zeta_{k}(\tau)\zeta_{k}^{*}(\tau_{2})^{2}\zeta_{q}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{k}^{\prime\,*}(\tau_{2})\zeta_{q}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})\right]+{\rm Im}\left[\zeta_{k}^{*}(\tau)\zeta_{k}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})\right]

\displaystyle{\rm Im}\left[\zeta_{k}(\tau)\zeta_{q}(\tau_{1})^{2}\zeta_{k}^{*}(\tau_{2})\zeta_{q}^{*}(\tau_{2})\zeta_{q}^{\prime\,*}(\tau_{2})\right]\}.

(6.72)

Again, since we are interested in modes
k
that are much smaller than the USR-enhanced ones, and are super-horizon at the time of USR phase, the contribution within the curly brackets in the first line vanishes thanks to eq. (
6.70
). This leaves us with

Contribution with one time derivatives on the long mode
k

\displaystyle\Delta P_{\rm 1st}(k,\tau)

\displaystyle\equiv-64\int_{\tau_{\rm in}}^{\tau}d\tau_{1}\epsilon(\tau_{1})\epsilon_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})a^{2}(\tau_{1})\int_{\tau_{\rm in}}^{\tau_{1}}d\tau_{2}\epsilon(\tau_{2})\epsilon_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{2})a^{2}(\tau_{2})\int_{q_{\rm in}}^{q_{\rm end}}dq\,q^{2}\,

\displaystyle\times{\rm Im}\left[\zeta_{k}^{*}(\tau)\zeta_{k}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})\right]{\rm Im}\left[\zeta_{k}(\tau)\zeta_{q}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{q}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{k}^{*}(\tau_{2})\zeta_{q}^{*}(\tau_{2})\zeta_{q}^{\prime\,*}(\tau_{2})\right].

(6.73)

•

2nd order in time derivatives of the long mode.

Analogue manipulations give

Contribution with
two time derivatives on the long mode
k

\displaystyle\Delta P_{\rm 2nd}(k,\tau)

\displaystyle\equiv-32\int_{\tau_{\rm in}}^{\tau}d\tau_{1}\epsilon(\tau_{1})\epsilon_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})a^{2}(\tau_{1})\int_{\tau_{\rm in}}^{\tau_{1}}d\tau_{2}\epsilon(\tau_{2})\epsilon_{2}^{\prime}(\tau_{2})a^{2}(\tau_{2})\int_{q_{\rm in}}^{q_{\rm end}}dq\,q^{2}\,

\displaystyle\times{\rm Im}\left[\zeta_{k}^{*}(\tau)\zeta_{k}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})\right]{\rm Im}\left[\zeta_{k}(\tau)\zeta_{q}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{q}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{k}^{\prime\,*}(\tau_{2})\zeta_{q}^{*}(\tau_{2})\zeta_{q}^{*}(\tau_{2})\right].

(6.74)

We stress that the only approximation employed so far is to take the external momentum to be much smaller than the one in the loop, i.e.
k\ll q
, which is justified in presence of a large hierarchy between the CMB and the USR scales.

Loop correction
at any scales

It will be useful in the following to remove the assumption that the external momentum is much smaller than the modes in the loop, i.e. the large separation of scales
k\ll q
.
Starting again from eqs. (
6.53
,
6.54
,
6.55
), we can proceed with analogous steps as in the previous section and define

\displaystyle X_{1}

\displaystyle\equiv\zeta_{k}^{*}(\tau)\zeta_{k}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})\,,\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ Y_{1}\equiv\zeta_{k}(\tau)\zeta_{k-q}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{q}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{k-q}^{*}(\tau_{2})\zeta_{k}^{\prime\,*}(\tau_{2})\zeta_{q}^{*}(\tau_{2})\,,

\displaystyle X_{2}

\displaystyle\equiv\zeta_{k}^{*}(\tau)\zeta_{k}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})\,,\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ Y_{2}\equiv\zeta_{k}(\tau)\zeta_{k-q}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{q}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{k}^{*}(\tau_{2})\big[\zeta_{k-q}^{\prime\,*}(\tau_{2})\zeta_{q}^{*}(\tau_{2})+\zeta_{k-q}^{*}(\tau_{2})\zeta_{q}^{\prime\,*}(\tau_{2})\big]\,,

\displaystyle X_{3}

\displaystyle\equiv\zeta_{k}^{*}(\tau)\zeta_{k}(\tau_{1})\,,\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ Y_{3}\equiv\zeta_{k}(\tau)\zeta_{k-q}^{*}(\tau_{2})\zeta^{\prime\,*}_{k}(\tau_{2})\zeta_{q}^{*}(\tau_{2})\big[\zeta_{k-q}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{q}(\tau_{1})+\zeta_{k-q}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{q}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})

\displaystyle X_{4}

\displaystyle\equiv\zeta_{k}^{*}(\tau)\zeta_{k}(\tau_{1})\,,\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ Y_{4}\equiv\zeta_{k}(\tau)\zeta_{k-q}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{q}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{k}^{*}(\tau_{2})\big[\zeta_{q}^{*}(\tau_{2})\zeta_{k-q}^{\prime\,*}(\tau_{2})+\zeta_{k-q}^{*}(\tau_{2})\zeta_{q}^{\prime\,*}(\tau_{2})\big]

\displaystyle X_{5}

\displaystyle\equiv\zeta_{k}^{*}(\tau)\zeta_{k}(\tau_{1})\,,\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ Y_{5}\equiv\zeta_{k}(\tau)\zeta_{k-q}^{\prime\,*}(\tau_{2})\zeta_{k}^{*}(\tau_{2})\zeta_{q}^{*}(\tau_{2})\big[\zeta_{q}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{k-q}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})+\zeta_{k-q}(\tau_{1})\zeta_{q}^{\prime}(\tau_{1})\big]

(6.75)

in such a way that
\Delta P\equiv\Delta P_{1}+\Delta P_{2}
can be written in the schematic form

Generic loop correction at any scale
k

\displaystyle\Delta P(k,\tau)

\displaystyle\equiv-16\int_{\tau_{\rm in}}^{\tau}d\tau_{1}g(\tau_{1})\int_{\tau_{\rm in}}^{\tau_{1}}d\tau_{2}g(\tau_{2})\int_{q_{\rm in}}^{q_{\rm end}}dq\,q^{2}\,\int_{-1}^{1}d(\cos\theta)\times\sum_{i=1}^{5}\Im(X_{i})\Im(Y_{i}),

(6.76)

thanks to the identity in eq. (
6.68
) and where we again introduced
\epsilon(\tau)\epsilon_{2}^{\prime}(\tau)a^{2}(\tau)\equiv g(\tau)
.
This expression is much more intricate than the one obtained in the limit of a large hierarchy of scales between the mode
k
and the USR loop momenta. It will allow us to seize the loop correction to the power spectrum also at the USR scales where the peak of the power spectrum is generated.

6.2.3
Time integration beyond the instantaneous transition and at any scales

Loop evaluation at the CMB scales

Let us try to simplify the structure of eq. (
6.53
) in light of the approximations introduced so far. First of all, let us write eq. (
6.53
)
in the form

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}(k)=\frac{H^{2}}{8\pi^{2}\epsilon_{\textrm{ref}}}\left\{1+\lim_{\tau\to 0^{-}}\frac{4\epsilon_{\textrm{ref}}k^{3}}{H^{2}(4\pi)^{2}}\Delta P_{\rm 1st}(k,\tau)+\lim_{\tau\to 0^{-}}\frac{4\epsilon_{\textrm{ref}}k^{3}}{H^{2}(4\pi)^{2}}\Delta P_{\rm 2nd}(k,\tau)\right\}\,,

(6.77)

where we used the slow-roll approximation for the first term in
eq. (
6.53
) given that

k
is of the order of the CMB pivot scale.
We focus on the leading correction given by
\Delta P_{\rm 1st}(k,\tau)
.
Using the number of
e
-folds as the time variable,
we find that it can be written in the compact form (cf. our definition in eq. (
6.28
))

\displaystyle\Delta\mathcal{P}_{\rm 1-loop}(k_{*})\equiv\lim_{\tau\to 0^{-}}\frac{4\epsilon_{\textrm{ref}}k^{3}}{H^{2}(4\pi)^{2}}\Delta P_{\rm 1st}(k,\tau)=

\displaystyle 32\left(\frac{H^{2}}{8\pi^{2}\epsilon_{\textrm{ref}}}\right)\int_{N_{\rm in}-\Delta N}^{N_{\rm end}+\Delta N}dN_{1}\frac{d\eta}{dN}(N_{1})\int_{N_{\rm in}-\Delta N}^{N_{1}}dN_{2}\bar{\epsilon}(N_{2})\frac{d\eta}{dN}(N_{2})e^{3(N_{2}-N_{\rm in})}\int\frac{d\bar{q}}{\bar{q}^{4}}{\rm Im}\left[\bar{\zeta}_{q}(N_{1})^{2}\bar{\zeta}_{q}^{*}(N_{2})\frac{d\bar{\zeta}_{q}^{*}}{dN}(N_{2})\right]

(6.78)

where we introduced the following manupulations:

i)

We use the approximation
\epsilon_{2}(N)\approx-2\eta(N)
.
This is because in the relevant range of
N
over which we integrate

\epsilon\ll 1
while
\eta=O(1)
, cf. the right panel of fig.
6.2
.

ii)

We define
\bar{q}\equiv q/a_{\textrm{in}}H
.
Furthermore, we use the two relations

\displaystyle\frac{a(N_{1})H}{k}=e^{N_{1}-N_{k}}\,,\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \textrm{with:}\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ a(N_{k})H=k\,,\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \textrm{and}\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \frac{a(N_{2})H}{q}=\frac{e^{N_{2}-N_{\rm in}}}{\bar{q}}\,.

(6.79)

iii)

We introduce the short-hand notation

\displaystyle\bar{\zeta}_{q}(N)\equiv\frac{\epsilon_{\textrm{ref}}^{1/2}q^{3/2}\zeta_{q}(N)}{H}\,.

(6.80)

The virtue of this definition is that
\bar{\zeta}_{q}(N)
is precisely the quantity we compute numerically by solving the M-S equation, cf. the right panel of fig.
6.3
.
Furthermore, it should be noted that
the definition in eq. (
6.80
) is automatically invariant under the rescaling in
eq. (
6.9
). The same comment applies to the definition of
\bar{q}
and the ratios in eq. (
6.79
). Consequently, an expression entirely written in terms of barred quantities is automatically invariant under the rescaling in
eq. (
6.9
).

iv)

Importantly, in the derivation of
eq. (
6.78
) we use (cf. appendix
C.1
)

\displaystyle\textrm{Im}\bigg[\bar{\zeta}_{k}^{*}(N)\frac{d\bar{\zeta}_{k}}{dN}(N_{1})\bigg]\simeq\textrm{Im}\bigg[\bar{\zeta}_{k}^{*}(N_{1})\frac{d\bar{\zeta}_{k}}{dN}(N_{1})\bigg]=-\frac{\bar{k}^{3}}{4\bar{\epsilon}(N_{1})}e^{3(N_{\textrm{in}}-N_{1})}\,,

(6.81)

with
\epsilon(N)
given by eq. (
6.23
) for generic
\delta N
.
This is because
N_{1,2}

vary at around
N_{\rm end}
,
and in this time interval modes with comoving wavenumbers
k\approx k_{*}

are way outside the horizon and stay constant.
For the very same reason, we also use the slow-roll approximation

\displaystyle\bar{\zeta}_{k}(N_{1})\bar{\zeta}^{*}_{k}(N_{2})=\frac{1}{4}\,.

(6.82)

v)

The range of integration in
eq. (
6.78
) is as follows.
In the case of a smooth transition, we take

N_{1}\in[N_{\textrm{in}}-\Delta N,N_{\textrm{end}}+\Delta N]
and

N_{2}\in[N_{\textrm{in}}-\Delta N,N_{1}]

where
\Delta N
should be large enough to complete the SR/USR/SR transition (that is,
\Delta N\gtrsim\delta N
).
In the limit of instantaneous transition, we set

N_{1}=N_{2}=N_{\rm end}
, which corresponds to consider the dominant contribution given by the first
\delta
function in eq. (
6.22
). Moreover, we include a factor
1/2
since, with respect to the integration over
N_{2}
, the argument of the
\delta
function in eq. (
6.22
) picks up the upper limit
of the integration interval at
N_{\rm end}
.
The integration over
q
, on the contrary, is limited by

\bar{q}\in[1,e^{\Delta N_{\textrm{USR}}}]
.

The instantaneous transition

We consider the instantaneous limit (dubbed
\delta N\to 0
in the following) of eq. (
6.78
). We find

\displaystyle\lim_{\delta N\to 0}\Delta\mathcal{P}_{\rm 1-loop}(k_{*})=\left(\frac{H^{2}}{8\pi^{2}\epsilon_{\textrm{ref}}}\right)\eta_{\textrm{II}}^{2}\left(\frac{k_{\textrm{end}}}{k_{\textrm{in}}}\right)^{-2\eta_{\textrm{II}}+3}\lim_{\delta N\to 0}(16)\int_{1}^{e^{\Delta N_{\textrm{USR}}}}\frac{d\bar{q}}{\bar{q}^{4}}|\bar{\zeta}_{q}(N_{\rm end})|^{2}{\rm Im}\left[\bar{\zeta}_{q}(N_{\rm end})\frac{d\bar{\zeta}_{q}^{*}}{dN}(N_{\rm end})\right]\,.

(6.83)

This expression can be further simplified using (cf. eq. (
C.13
))

\displaystyle{\rm Im}\left[\bar{\zeta}_{q}(N_{\rm end})\frac{d\bar{\zeta}_{q}^{*}}{dN}(N_{\rm end})\right]=\frac{\bar{q}^{3}}{4}e^{(2\eta_{\textrm{II}}-3)(N_{\textrm{end}}-N_{\textrm{in}})}=\frac{\bar{q}^{3}}{4}\left(\frac{k_{\rm end}}{k_{\rm in}}\right)^{2\eta_{\rm II}-3}\,,

(6.84)

so that we write

\displaystyle\lim_{\delta N\to 0}\Delta\mathcal{P}_{\rm 1-loop}(k_{*})=\left(\frac{H^{2}}{8\pi^{2}\epsilon_{\textrm{ref}}}\right)\,4\eta_{\textrm{II}}^{2}\,\lim_{\delta N\to 0}\int_{1}^{e^{\Delta N_{\textrm{USR}}}}\frac{d\bar{q}}{\bar{q}}|\bar{\zeta}_{q}(N_{\rm end})|^{2}\,.

(6.85)

We remark that this expression is valid for generic values of
\eta_{\rm II}
during USR.

We consider now the computation of the last integral.
The factor

\bar{\zeta}_{q}
grows exponentially during the
USR phase.
In the case of sub-horizon modes, we have
\bar{\zeta}_{q}(N)\sim e^{-(1-\eta_{\rm II})N}
while in the case of super-horizon modes we find
\bar{\zeta}_{q}(N)\sim e^{-(3-2\eta_{\rm II})N}
(cf. appendix
C.1
).
However, the precise estimate of the integral in eq. (
6.85
) is complicated by the fact that
curvature modes
\bar{\zeta}_{q}
with
\bar{q}\in[1,\exp(\Delta N_{\textrm{USR}})]
are neither sub- nor super-horizon but they exit the horizon during the USR phase, thus making the analytical estimate of the argument of their exponential growth more challenging.

The situation simplifies if we consider some special values of
\eta_{\textrm{II}}
.
We consider the case
\eta_{\textrm{II}}=3

(that is
\epsilon_{2}=-6
, it should be noted that this is also the case studied in ref.
[
683
]
).
In this case, everything can be computed analytically.
We find the scaling

\displaystyle\lim_{\delta N\to 0}\int_{1}^{e^{\Delta N_{\textrm{USR}}}}\frac{d\bar{q}}{\bar{q}}|\bar{\zeta}_{q}(N_{\rm end})|^{2}\approx\frac{e^{6\Delta N_{\rm USR}}}{4}(1+\Delta N_{\rm USR})=\frac{1}{4}\left(\frac{k_{\rm end}}{k_{\rm in}}\right)^{6}\left[1+\log\left(\frac{k_{\rm end}}{k_{\rm in}}\right)\right]\,,

(6.86)

which becomes more and more accurate for larger
k_{\rm end}/k_{\rm in}
.
The final result is

Leading one-loop correction at CMB scales in the instantaneous SR/USR/SR transition

\displaystyle\lim_{\delta N\to 0}\Delta\mathcal{P}_{\rm 1-loop}(k_{*})\approx\left(\frac{H^{2}}{8\pi^{2}\epsilon_{\textrm{ref}}}\right)\eta_{\rm II}^{2}\left(\frac{k_{\textrm{end}}}{k_{\textrm{in}}}\right)^{6}\left[1+\log\left(\frac{k_{\rm end}}{k_{\rm in}}\right)\right]\,,\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \textrm{with}\penalty\ \penalty\ \eta_{\textrm{II}}=3,\,\,\eta_{\rm III}=0

(6.87)

which perfectly agrees with the findings of ref.
[
683
]
in the same limit.

The above result has a number of limitations, which we address separately:

\circ

Dynamics during USR.

We modify the assumption
\eta_{\rm II}=3
and we take
\delta N\to 0
and
\eta_{\rm III}=0
.

\circ

Dynamics at the SR/USR/SR transition.

We consider
\delta N\neq 0
, with generic
\eta_{\rm II}
but
\eta_{\rm III}=0
.
Considering a non-zero value of
\delta N
is very important because it corresponds to a more realistic smooth SR/USR/SR transition, as opposed to the instantaneous limit with
\delta N=0
.

Dynamics during USR

We compute eq. (
6.85
) for generic values of
\eta_{\rm II}
, still keeping
\delta N\to 0
and
\eta_{\rm III}=0
.
From the computation of the tree-level power spectrum (see fig.
6.3
) we define

\displaystyle\frac{\mathcal{P}_{\rm USR}}{\mathcal{P}_{\rm CMB}}\equiv\frac{\mathcal{P}(\bar{k}_{\rm max})}{\mathcal{P}(\bar{k}\ll 1)}\,,

(6.88)

where
\bar{k}_{\rm max}
represent the position of the max of
\mathcal{P}(\bar{k})
after the growth
due to the USR dynamics.

Figure 6.4:

In both panels,
we consider a generic USR dynamics with varying
\eta_{\rm II}
(
x
-axis) and
\Delta N_{\rm USR}
(
y
-axis). We take
\eta_{\rm III}=0
and the instantaneous limit
\delta N=0
.
We plot in solid red
contours of constant
\lim_{\delta N\to 0}\Delta\mathcal{P}_{\rm 1-loop}(k_{*}),

defined in eq. (
6.78
) and
computed according to eq. (
6.85
) with
H^{2}/8\pi^{2}\epsilon_{\rm ref}=2.1\times 10^{-9}
.
The region hatched in red
is defined by the condition
\lim_{\delta N\to 0}\Delta\mathcal{P}_{\rm 1-loop}(k_{*})>1
.

Left panel:
We superimpose contours
of constant
\mathcal{P}_{\rm USR}/\mathcal{P}_{\rm CMB}
as defined in eq. (
6.88
) (dashed blue).

Right panel:
We superimpose the line defined by the condition
f_{\rm PBH}=1
.
Along this line, we get 100% of DM in the form of asteroid-mass PBHs.

We compare in the left panel of fig.
6.4
contours
of constant
\mathcal{P}_{\rm USR}/\mathcal{P}_{\rm CMB}
(dashed blue) and constant
\lim_{\delta N\to 0}\Delta\mathcal{P}_{\rm 1-loop}(k_{*})
(solid red).
We take
H^{2}/8\pi^{2}\epsilon_{\rm ref}=2.1\times 10^{-9}
.
Our analysis shows that enhancements

\mathcal{P}_{\rm USR}/\mathcal{P}_{\rm CMB}\gtrsim 10^{8}
are barely compatible with the perturbativity condition

\lim_{\delta N\to 0}\Delta\mathcal{P}_{\rm 1-loop}(k_{*})<1
, which roughly means “loops
<
tree level”. The region
\lim_{\delta N\to 0}\Delta\mathcal{P}_{\rm 1-loop}(k_{*})>1
is hatched in red in fig.
6.4
.

We can actually do better and compare with a careful computation of the PBH abundance.
The parameters of the dynamics, using the reverse engineering approach, (with
\eta_{\rm III}=0
and
\delta N\to 0
) are chosen in such a way that the integral

\displaystyle f_{\rm PBH}\equiv\frac{\Omega_{\rm PBH}}{\Omega_{\rm CDM}}=\int f_{\rm PBH}(M_{\rm PBH})d\log M_{\rm PBH}\approx 1\,,

(6.89)

which means that we get
\approx 100\%
of DM in the form of PBHs.
More in detail, we tune, for each
\eta_{\rm II}
, the value of
\Delta N_{\rm USR}
so to get
f_{\rm PBH}\approx 1
;
we choose
the numerical value of
k_{\rm in}
in such a way that the peak of the PBH mass distribution
f_{\rm PBH}(M_{\rm PBH})
falls within the interval
M_{\rm PBH}/M_{\odot}\in[10^{-14},\,10^{-13}]
in which the condition

f_{\rm PBH}\approx 1
is experimentally allowed, the so-called asteroid-mass PBHs
[
706
]
.
We compute eq. (
6.89
) using threshold statistics and including the full non-linear relation between the curvature and the density contrast fields (cf.
[
220
,
219
]
) (see chapter.2) but assuming a gaussian curvature field
\zeta
4
4

4

It is possible to further improve our analysis by including the presence of primordial non-gaussianity (e.g.
[
707
,
708
,
709
,
710
,
711
,
712
,
261
,
713
,
248
]
).
In the case of local non-guassianity parametrized by a positive non-Guassian parameter
f_{\rm NL}
, as expected in the case of USR, we generically expect a larger abundance of PBHs compared to the Gaussian case
[
247
,
1
,
277
,
3
,
6
]
.
This means that, in order to achieve the same abundance of PBHs, one needs a power spectrum with a smaller peak amplitude. This argument implies that the presence of primordial non-Gaussianity will tend to decrease the relevance of the one-loop corrections.

.
In the right panel of fig.
6.4
we
plot the line defined by the condition
f_{\rm PBH}\approx 1
. The comparison
